Fully Evaluated Left-Sequential Logics
Alban Ponse & Daan J.C. Staudt

Abstract
We consider a family of two-valued “fully evaluated left-sequential logics” (FELs), of which Free FEL (defined by Staudt in 2012) is most distinguishing (weakest) and immune to atomic side effects. Next is Memorising FEL, in which evaluations of subexpressions are memorised. The following stronger logic is Conditional FEL (inspired by Guzmán and Squier’s Conditional logic, 1990). The strongest FEL is static FEL, a sequential version of propositional logic. We use evaluation trees as a simple, intuitive semantics and provide complete axiomatisations for closed terms (left-sequential propositional expressions). For each FEL except Static FEL, we also define its three-valued version, with a constant U for “undefinedness” and again provide complete, independent axiomatisations, each one containing two additional axioms for U on top of the axiomatisations of the two-valued case. In this setting, the strongest FEL is equivalent to Bochvar’s strict logic. Keywords & phrases: full left-sequential evaluation, strict evaluation, left-sequential connectives, Bochvar’s logic



1
Introduction

This paper has its origin in the work of Staudt
[
19
]
,
in which so called “Free Fully Evaluated Left-Sequential Logic” (
FFEL
) was introduced,
together with “evaluation trees” as a simple semantics and an equational axiomatisation
of their equality.

We define a family of “Fully Evaluated Left-Sequential Logics” (
FEL
s) that is about full
left-sequential evaluation (also called strict evaluation) and of which
FFEL
is the most distinguishing
(weakest).
As in
[
19
]
, we use for the Boolean connectives a dedicated notation that prescribes
a full, left-sequential evaluation strategy: we consider terms
(propositional expressions) that are built from
atoms
a,b,c,...
(propositional variables) and constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
for truth and falsehood by composition with negation (
\neg
) and
sequential connectives: left-sequential conjunction, notation
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}
, and left-sequential
disjunction, notation
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}
, where the little black dot
denotes that the left argument
must be evaluated first, and then the right argument.
For two-valued
FEL
s we discern a hierarchy starting with

FFEL
, which is immune to (atomic) side effects,
and ending with a sequential version of propositional logic.

Evaluation trees
are binary trees with internal nodes labeled by atoms and
leaves labeled with either
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
, and provide a simple semantics for propositional expressions:
a path from the root to a leaf models an evaluation.
The left branch from an internal node indicates that its atom is evaluated
true
, and the right
branch that the atom evaluates to
false
.
The leaves of an evaluation tree represent evaluation results.
Atom
a
has as its semantics the evaluation tree
a
{\sf T}
{\sf F}

and for example,
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}b

has as its semantics the evaluation tree
a
b
{\sf T}
{\sf F}
b
{\sf F}
{\sf F}

which is composed from the evaluation trees of atoms
a
and
b
.
If in
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}b
, atom
a
evaluates to
true
, then atom
b
is evaluated and determines the
evaluation result, and if
a
evaluates to
false
, then the evaluation result
is
false
, but
b
is (still) evaluated.
Note that in the example tree of
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}b
, replacing
b
by
a
has an evaluation tree that
is
not
equal to that of
a
; the evaluation of the first
a
can have a side effect that
changes the second evaluation result.

In
[
19
]
, evaluation trees were introduced
as the basis of a relatively simple semantic framework, both for
FFEL
and
for so-called
Free Short-Circuit Logic
(FSCL)
[
4
]
,
1
1

1

In “short-circuit logics” (SCLs), short-circuit evaluation is prescribed by the
short-circuit connectives
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\wedge$}\put(-0.54,-0.2){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,-0.2){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
and
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.54,1.54){{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\circle*{0.6}}}\put(-0.54,1.54){\circle{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
; in the journal paper
[
18
]
,
the FSCL-part of
[
19
]
is also discussed.

and completeness results for both
FFEL
and FSCL were provided, i.e. equational axiomatisations
of the equality of their evaluation trees.
For example,
FFEL
refutes the idempotence axiom
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x=x
,
but satisfies the associativity of the binary connectives.
In the subsequent paper
[
2
]
, transformations on evaluation trees were defined in order to
provide a semantic basis for other short-circuit logics, see
[
18
,
6
,
3
]
.
In this paper, we introduce three new FELs with associated evaluation trees, each of which has a
counterpart in short-circuit logic, and give complete, equational axiomatisations:

Memorising FEL (
MFEL
).

The logic
MFEL
characterises the evaluation strategy in which the
evaluation of each atom in an expression is memorised:
no complete path in a “memorising evaluation tree” contains multiple occurrences of the same atom.
For example,
a
and
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}a
have the same memorising evaluation tree (that of
a
).
In
MFEL
, atoms cannot have side effects and the evaluation order of a propositional expression
is prescribed. As an example, the memorising evaluation trees of
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}b
and
b~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}a
are
different.

MFEL
is axiomatised by adding one equational axiom to those of
FFEL
.

Conditional FEL (C
\bm{\ell}
FEL
2
).

The name
C
\ell
FEL
2
refers to Conditional logic, defined by Guzmán and Squier
[
12
]
,
and the subscript 2 refers to its two-valued version. In
C
\ell
FEL
2
,

{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
and
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
are taken to be commutative.
In
[
3
]
, we show that this
commutativity is a consequence of Conditional logic.

C
\ell
FEL
2
-evaluation trees are memorising evaluation trees in which the order of
commuting atoms can be changed while preserving equivalence.

C
\ell
FEL
2
is axiomatised by the axioms of
MFEL
and
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x
.

Static FEL (
SFEL
).

This logic is just a sequential version of propositional logic.

SFEL
is axiomatised by adding the axiom
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}={\sf F}
to those of
C
\ell
FEL
2
.

Evaluation trees for full left-sequential evaluation can be easily extended to a three-valued
case by including

{\sf U}
as a leaf, which represents the truth value
undefined
.
Evaluation trees with leaves in
\{{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U}\}

were introduced in
[
6
]
, where they serve as a semantics for
short-circuit logic with undefinedness.
In both
FEL
and
SCL
, atom
a
has as its semantics the left tree below, which shows that
the evaluation of each atom can be
undefined
. Another example in
FEL
is the
evaluation tree of

a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(b~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf U})
, shown on the right:

The general idea here is that in propositional expressions,
{\sf U}
aborts further evaluation.
This property can be equationally axiomatised by
\neg{\sf U}={\sf U}
and
{\sf U}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x={\sf U}

(and by duality it follows that

{\sf U}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x={\sf U}
).
A programming-oriented example is a condition
\texttt{[y != 0]}\>~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\>\texttt{[x/y > 17]}
,
where the atom
[x/y > 17]
can be undefined.

For each
FEL
except
SFEL
, we define the three-valued version and give
equational axiomatisations consisting of those for
the two-valued case and the two mentioned axioms for
{\sf U}
.
In this family,
C
\ell
FEL
with undefinedness
is equivalent to Bochvar’s strict logic
[
8
]
and
{\sf U}
is fully absorptive.
Note that
SFEL
cannot be extended with
{\sf U}
:
{\sf F}={\sf U}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}={\sf U}
.

We then present alternative, independent axiomatisations for these equational axiomatisations
that are simpler and more concise.
We end the paper with some remarks on our specific notation for left-sequential connectives,
expressiveness, and related work.

Prover9
and
Mace4
.

Apart from inductive proofs, all presented derivabilities from
equational axiomatisations were found by or
checked with the theorem
prover
Prover9
, and finite (counter) models were generated with the tool
Mace4
.
For both tools, see
[
16
]
. We used these tools on a Macbook Pro with
a 2.4 GHz dual-core Intel Core i5 processor and 4GB of RAM.
Average running times are mostly
given in mere seconds and rounded up (for example, 2s).

Structure of the paper.

In Section
2
, we review Free FEL (
FFEL
) and its equational axiomatisation.
In Section
3
, we define
\textup{$\textrm{FFEL}$}^{\sf U}
, the extension of
FFEL
with undefinedness.
In Section
4
we define Memorising FEL (
MFEL
) and provide an equational axiomatisation,
and in Section
5
we extend
MFEL
to
\textup{$\textrm{MFEL}$}^{\sf U}
with undefinedness.
In Section
6
, we define two-valued Conditional FEL (
C
\ell
FEL
2
) and its extension

C
\ell
FEL
with undefinedness.
In Section
7
, we briefly consider Static FEL (
SFEL
), and then
provide for each
FEL
a set of independent axioms.
Section
8
contains some discussion and conclusions.

2
Free FEL (
FFEL
) and an equational axiomatisation

In this section, we review evaluation trees and the logic
FFEL
.
We repeat the main results about
FFEL
, that is, its evaluation trees and axiomatisation, and
the completeness proof as presented in
[
19
]
.

From this point on, we assume that
A
is a countable set of atoms with typical elements
a
,
b
,
c
.
We start with a formal definition of evaluation trees.

Definition 2.1
.

The set
\mathcal{T}_{A}
of
evaluation trees
over
A
with leaves in

\{{\sf T},{\sf F}\}
is defined inductively by

{\sf T}\in\mathcal{T}_{A},\hskip 10.00002pt{\sf F}\in\mathcal{T}_{A},\hskip 10.00002pt(X\unlhd a\unrhd Y)\in\mathcal{T}_{A}~\text{ for any }X,Y\in\mathcal{T}_{A}\text{ and }a\in A.

The operator
\_\unlhd a\unrhd\_
is called

tree composition over
a
.
In the evaluation tree
X\unlhd a\unrhd Y
,
the root is represented by
a
,
the left branch by
X
and the right branch by
Y
.

The
depth

d:\mathcal{T}_{A}\to\mathbb{N}
of an evaluation tree is defined by

d({\sf T})=d({\sf F})=0
and

d(Y\unlhd a\unrhd Z)=1+\max(d(Y),d(Z))
.

The leaves of an evaluation tree represent evaluation results (we use
the constants
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
for
true
and
false
).
Next to the formal notation for evaluation
trees we also use a more pictorial representation. For example,
the tree

({\sf F}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F})\unlhd b\unrhd({\sf T}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F})

can be represented as follows, where
\unlhd
yields a left branch, and
\unrhd
a right branch:

b
a
{\sf F}
{\sf F}
a
{\sf T}
{\sf F}

(Picture 1)

In order to define a semantics for full evaluation of negation and the left-sequential
connectives, we first define the
leaf replacement
operator,
‘replacement’ for short, on trees in
\mathcal{T}_{A}
as follows.
For
X\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
, the replacement of
{\sf T}
with
Y
and
{\sf F}
with
Z
in
X
, denoted

X[{\sf T}\mapsto Y,{\sf F}\mapsto Z]

is defined recursively by

\displaystyle{\sf T}[{\sf T}\mapsto Y,{\sf F}\mapsto Z]

\displaystyle=Y,

\displaystyle{\sf F}[{\sf T}\mapsto Y,{\sf F}\mapsto Z]

\displaystyle=Z,

\displaystyle(X_{1}\unlhd a\unrhd X_{2})[{\sf T}\mapsto Y,{\sf F}\mapsto Z]

\displaystyle=X_{1}[{\sf T}\mapsto Y,{\sf F}\mapsto Z]\unlhd a\unrhd X_{2}[{\sf T}\mapsto Y,{\sf F}\mapsto Z].

We note that the order in which the replacements of leaves of

X
is listed is irrelevant and adopt the convention of not listing
identities inside the brackets, e.g.,

X[{\sf F}\mapsto Z]=X[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf T},{\sf F}\mapsto Z]
.
By structural induction it follows that repeated replacements satisfy

X[{\sf T}\mapsto Y_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto Z_{1}][{\sf T}\mapsto Y_{2},{\sf F}\mapsto Z_{2}]=X[{\sf T}\mapsto Y_{1}[{\sf T}\mapsto Y_{2},{\sf F}\mapsto Z_{2}],~{\sf F}\mapsto Z_{1}[{\sf T}\mapsto Y_{2},{\sf F}\mapsto Z_{2}]].

The set
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
of (left-sequential) propositional expressions over
A

that prescribe full left-sequential evaluation is defined
by the following grammar (
a\in A
):

P::={\sf T}\mid{\sf F}\mid a\mid\neg P\mid P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P\mid P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P,

and we refer to its signature by

\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{FEL}$}}(A)=\{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},\neg,{\sf T},{\sf F},a\mid a\in A\}.

We interpret expressions in
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
as evaluation trees
by a function
\mathit{fe}
(abbreviating full evaluation).

Definition 2.2
.

The unary
full evaluation
function
\mathit{fe}:{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}\to\mathcal{T}_{A}

is defined as
follows, where
a\in A
:

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}({\sf T})

\displaystyle={\sf T},

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(\neg P)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F},{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}({\sf F})

\displaystyle={\sf F},

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]],

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(a)

\displaystyle={\sf T}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F},

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)].

The overloading of the symbol
{\sf T}
in
\mathit{fe}({\sf T})={\sf T}
will not
cause confusion (and similarly for
{\sf F}
).
As a simple example we derive the evaluation tree of
\neg b~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}a
:

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(\neg b~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}a)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(\neg b)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(a),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(a)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]

\displaystyle=({\sf F}\unlhd b\unrhd{\sf T})[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(a),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(a)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]

\displaystyle=({\sf F}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F})\unlhd b\unrhd({\sf T}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F}),

which can be visualized as in
Picture 1
.
Also,
\mathit{fe}(\neg(b~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg a))=({\sf F}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F})\unlhd b\unrhd({\sf T}\unlhd a\unrhd{\sf F})
.
An evaluation tree
\mathit{fe}(P)
represents full evaluation in a way that can be
compared to the notion of a truth table for propositional logic in that it
represents each possible evaluation of
P
. However, there are some important differences with
truth tables: in
\mathit{fe}(P)
, the sequentiality
of
P
’s evaluation is represented,
the same atom may occur multiple times in
\mathit{fe}(P)
with different evaluation values, and all atoms
are evaluated, thus yielding
evaluation trees that are perfect. Evaluation trees that are
not perfect occur in the setting with

short-circuit
connectives; in that setting, the connectives
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
and
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}

are definable (we return to this in Sections
7
and
8
).

\displaystyle{\sf F}

\displaystyle=\neg{\sf T}

(FFEL1)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

\displaystyle=\neg(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg y)

(FFEL2)

\displaystyle\neg\neg x

\displaystyle=x

(FFEL3)

\displaystyle(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}z

\displaystyle=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}z)

(FFEL4)

\displaystyle{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x

\displaystyle=x

(FFEL5)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}

\displaystyle=x

(FFEL6)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}

\displaystyle={\sf F}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}x

(FFEL7)

\displaystyle\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}

\displaystyle=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}

(FFEL8)

\displaystyle(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

\displaystyle=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

(FFEL9)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})

\displaystyle=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})

(FFEL10)

Table 1:
EqFFEL
, axioms for
FFEL

Definition 2.3
.

The binary relation
=_{\mathit{fe}}

on
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
defined by
P=_{\mathit{fe}}Q\iff\mathit{fe}(P)=\mathit{fe}(Q)

is called
free full valuation congruence
.

Lemma 2.4
.

The relation
=_{\mathit{fe}}
is a congruence.

Proof.

It is immediately clear that identity, symmetry and transitivity are preserved.
For congruence we show only that for all
P,Q,R\in{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
,
P=_{\mathit{fe}}Q
implies
R~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P=_{\mathit{fe}}R~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
. The other cases proceed
in a similar fashion. If
\mathit{fe}(P)=\mathit{fe}(Q)
then

\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]=\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]
, so

\mathit{fe}(R)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(P),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]=\mathit{fe}(R)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]].

Therefore, by definition of
\mathit{fe}
,
R~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P=_{\mathit{fe}}R~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
.
∎

Definition 2.5
.

A
Fully Evaluated Left-Sequential Logic
(
FEL
)
is a logic that satisfies the
consequences of
\mathit{fe}
-equality.

Free Fully Evaluated Left-Sequential Logic
(
FFEL
)
is the fully evaluated
left-sequential logic that satisfies no more consequences than those of
\mathit{fe}
-equality, i.e.,
for all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
,

\textup{$\textrm{FEL}$}\models P=Q\Longleftarrow P=_{\mathit{fe}}Q\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\textup{$\textrm{FFEL}$}\models P=Q\iff P=_{\mathit{fe}}Q.

In
[
19
]
, it is proven that the set
EqFFEL
of equational axioms in Table
1

(completely) axiomatises equality of evaluation trees; this proof is based on normal forms for
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
.
Below, we repeat the completeness proof of
[
19
]
almost verbatim.
In the remainder of this paper we define several equational logics that model sequential
full evaluation according to various evaluation strategies and the
corresponding adaptations of evaluation trees. A general reference to equational
logics is
[
9
]
.

The following lemma shows some useful equations illustrating the special
properties of terms of the form
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}
and
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}
. The
first is an ‘extension’ of axiom (
FFEL8
) and the others show two different
ways how terms of the form
x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}
, and by duality terms of the form

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}
, can change the main connective of a term.

Lemma 2.6
.

The following equations are consequences of
EqFFEL
.

1.

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})
,

2.

(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y=\neg(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y
,

3.

x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(z~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}))=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(z~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})
.

Proof.

We derive the equations in order.

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})

\displaystyle=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

by (
FFEL7
) and (
FFEL4
)

\displaystyle=(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

by (
FFEL8
)

\displaystyle=\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}),

by (
FFEL7
) and (
FFEL4
)

\displaystyle(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

\displaystyle=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

by (
FFEL9
)

\displaystyle=(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

by (
FFEL8
)

\displaystyle=(\neg x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}\neg{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y

by (
FFEL1
)

\displaystyle=\neg(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y,

by (
FFEL3
) and (
FFEL2
)

\displaystyle x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(z~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}))

\displaystyle=x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(y~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(z~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}))

by (
FFEL10
)

\displaystyle=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(z~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})

by the dual of (
FFEL4
)

\displaystyle=(x~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}y)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(z~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}).

by (
FFEL10
)

∎

We now turn to the equational logic defined by
EqFFEL
, which we will show is an
axiomatization of
FFEL
. This set of equations was first presented by Blok in

[
7
]
.

If two
FEL
-terms
s
and
t
, possibly containing variables, are
derivable in
EqFFEL
, we write
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash s=t
and say that
s
and
t
are derivably equal. By virtue of
(
FFEL1
) through (
FFEL3
),
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
is the dual of
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}

and
{\sf T}
is the dual of
{\sf F}
,
and hence the duals of the equations in
EqFFEL
are also derivable. We will
use this fact implicitly throughout our proofs.

Lemma 2.7

(Soundness)
.

For all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
, if
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash P=Q
then
\textup{$\textrm{FFEL}$}\models P=Q
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4
,
=_{\mathit{fe}}
is a congruence, so it suffices to prove the validity of
the equations in
EqFFEL
, which is easily verified. As an example we show this for (
FFEL8
).

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F},{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf F}[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]

by definition

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]

because
{\sf F}[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]={\sf F}

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F},{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}][{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]

by induction

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(\neg P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F}),

where the induction that proves the third equality is on the structure of
evaluation trees.
∎

FEL Normal Form.

To aid in our completeness proof we define a normal form for
FEL
-terms. Due
to the possible presence of side effects,
FFEL
does not identify terms which
contain different atoms or the same atoms in a different order. Because of
this, common normal forms for propositional logic are not normal forms for
FEL
-terms. For
example, rewriting a term to Conjunctive Normal Form or Disjunctive Normal Form
may require duplicating some of the atoms in the term, thus yielding a term
that is not derivably equal to the original. We first present the grammar for
our normal form, before motivating it. The normal form we present here is an
adaptation of a normal form proposed by Blok in
[
7
]
.

Definition 2.8
.

A term
P\in{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
is said to be in
FEL
Normal Form
(\textup{FNF})
if it
is generated by the following grammar.

\displaystyle P\in\textup{FNF}

\displaystyle::=P^{\sf T}~\mid~P^{\sf F}~\mid~P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{*}

\displaystyle P^{*}

\displaystyle::=P^{c}~\mid~P^{d}

\displaystyle P^{c}

\displaystyle::=P^{\ell}~\mid~P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{d}

\displaystyle P^{d}

\displaystyle::=P^{\ell}~\mid~P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{c}

\displaystyle P^{\ell}

\displaystyle::=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T}~\mid~\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T}

\displaystyle P^{\sf T}

\displaystyle::={\sf T}~\mid~a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T}

\displaystyle P^{\sf F}

\displaystyle::={\sf F}~\mid~a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf F},

where
a\in A
. We refer to
P^{*}
-forms as
*
-terms, to
P^{\ell}
-forms as

\ell
-terms, to
P^{\sf T}
-forms as
{\sf T}
-terms and to
P^{\sf F}
-forms as

{\sf F}
-terms. A term of the form
P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{*}
is referred to as a

{\sf T}
-
*
-term.

We immediately note that if it were not for the presence of
{\sf T}
and

{\sf F}
we could define a much simpler normal form. In that case it would
suffice to ‘push in’ or ‘push down’ the negations, thus obtaining a Negation
Normal Form (NNF), as exists for propositional logic. Naturally if our set
A
of atoms is empty, the
truth value constants would be a normal form.

When considering the image of
\mathit{fe}
we note that some trees only have

{\sf T}
-leaves, some only have
{\sf F}
-leaves and some have both

{\sf T}
-leaves and
{\sf F}
-leaves. For any
FEL
-term
P
,
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})
is a tree with only
{\sf T}
-leaves, as can easily be seen from the
definition of
\mathit{fe}
. All terms
P
such that
\mathit{fe}(P)
only has
{\sf T}
-leaves
are rewritten to
{\sf T}
-terms. Similarly
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})
is a tree
with only
{\sf F}
-leaves. All terms
P
such that
\mathit{fe}(P)
only has

{\sf F}
-leaves are rewritten to
{\sf F}
-terms. The simplest trees in the
image of
\mathit{fe}
that have both
{\sf T}
-leaves and
{\sf F}
-leaves are
\mathit{fe}(a)

for
a\in A
. Any (occurrence of an) atom that determines (in whole or in part)
the evaluation result of a term, such as
a
in this example, is referred to as a
determinative (occurrence of an) atom. This as opposed to a non-determinative
(occurrence of an) atom, such as the
a
in
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T}
, which does not
determine (either in whole or in part) the evaluation result of the term. Note that a term

P
such that
\mathit{fe}(P)
contains both
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
must contain at least
one determinative atom.

Terms that contain at least one determinative atom will be rewritten to

{\sf T}
-
*
-terms. In
{\sf T}
-
*
-terms we encode each determinative atom
together with the non-determinative atoms that occur between it and the next
determinative atom in the term (reading from left to right) as an
\ell
-term.
Observe that the first atom in an
\ell
-term is the (only) determinative atom
in that
\ell
-term and that determinative atoms only occur in
\ell
-terms.
Also observe that the evaluation result of an
\ell
-term is that of its determinative
atom. This is intuitively convincing, because the remainder of the atoms in any

\ell
-term are non-determinative and hence do not contribute to its evaluation result.
The non-determinative atoms that may occur before the first determinative atom are
encoded as a
{\sf T}
-term. A
{\sf T}
-
*
-term is the conjunction of a

{\sf T}
-term encoding such atoms and a
*
-term, which contains only
conjunctions and disjunctions of
\ell
-terms. We could also have encoded such
atoms as an
{\sf F}
-term and then taken the disjunction with a
*
-term to
obtain a term with the same semantics. We consider
\ell
-terms to be ‘basic’
in
*
-terms in the sense that they are the smallest grammatical unit that
influences the evaluation result of the
*
-term.

In the following,
P^{\sf T},P^{\ell}
, etc. are used both to denote grammatical
categories and as variables for terms in those categories. The remainder of
this section is concerned with defining and proving correct the normalization
function
f:{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}\to\textup{FNF}
. We will define
f
recursively using the
functions

f^{n}:\textup{FNF}\to\textup{FNF}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptf^{c}:\textup{FNF}\times\textup{FNF}\to\textup{FNF}.

The first of these will be used to rewrite negated
FNF
-terms to
FNF
-terms
and the second to rewrite the conjunction of two
FNF
-terms to an

FNF
-term. By (
FFEL2
) we have no need for a dedicated function that
rewrites the disjunction of two
FNF
-terms to an
FNF
-term.

We start by defining
f^{n}
. Analysing the semantics of
{\sf T}
-terms and

{\sf F}
-terms together with the definition of
\mathit{fe}
on negations, it becomes
clear that
f^{n}
must turn
{\sf T}
-terms into
{\sf F}
-terms and vice versa.
We also remark that
f^{n}
must preserve the left-associativity of the

*
-terms in
{\sf T}
-
*
-terms, modulo the associativity within
\ell
-terms.
We define
f^{n}:\textup{FNF}\to\textup{FNF}
as follows, using the auxiliary function

f^{n}_{1}:P^{*}\to P^{*}
to ‘push down’ or ‘push in’ the negation symbols when
negating a
{\sf T}
-
*
-term. We note that there is no ambiguity between the
different grammatical categories present in an
FNF
-term, i.e., any

FNF
-term is in exactly one of the grammatical categories identified in
Definition
2.8
.

\displaystyle f^{n}({\sf T})

\displaystyle={\sf F}

(1)

\displaystyle f^{n}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{n}(P^{\sf T})

(2)

\displaystyle f^{n}({\sf F})

\displaystyle={\sf T}

(3)

\displaystyle f^{n}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{n}(P^{\sf F}).

(4)

\displaystyle f^{n}(P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*})

\displaystyle=P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{n}_{1}(Q^{*})

(5)

\displaystyle f^{n}_{1}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T}

(6)

\displaystyle f^{n}_{1}(\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T}

(7)

\displaystyle f^{n}_{1}(P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{d})

\displaystyle=f^{n}_{1}(P^{*})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{n}_{1}(Q^{d})

(8)

\displaystyle f^{n}_{1}(P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{c})

\displaystyle=f^{n}_{1}(P^{*})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{n}_{1}(Q^{c})

(9)

Now we turn to defining
f^{c}
. These definitions have a great deal of
inter-dependence so we first present the definition for
f^{c}
when the first
argument is a
{\sf T}
-term. We see that the conjunction of a
{\sf T}
-term with
another term always yields a term of the same grammatical category as the
second conjunct.

\displaystyle f^{c}({\sf T},P)

\displaystyle=P

(10)

\displaystyle f^{c}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})

(11)

\displaystyle f^{c}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf F})

(12)

\displaystyle f^{c}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R^{*})

\displaystyle=f^{c}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R^{*}

(13)

For defining
f^{c}
where the first argument is an
{\sf F}
-term we make use
of (
FFEL7
) when dealing with conjunctions of
{\sf F}
-terms with

{\sf T}
-
*
-terms. The definition of
f^{c}
for the arguments used in the
right hand side of (
16
) starts at (
23
). We note that
despite the high level of inter-dependence in these definitions, this does not
create a circular definition. We also note that the conjunction of an

{\sf F}
-term with another term is always itself an
{\sf F}
-term.

\displaystyle f^{c}({\sf F},P^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=f^{n}(P^{\sf T})

(14)

\displaystyle f^{c}({\sf F},P^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=P^{\sf F}

(15)

\displaystyle f^{c}({\sf F},P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*})

\displaystyle=f^{c}(P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*},{\sf F})

(16)

\displaystyle f^{c}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf F},Q)

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf F},Q)

(17)

The case where the first conjunct is a
{\sf T}
-
*
-term is the most
complicated. Therefore we first consider the case where the second conjunct is
a
{\sf T}
-term. In this case we must make the
{\sf T}
-term part of the last
(rightmost)
\ell
-term in the
{\sf T}
-
*
-term, so that the result will
again be a
{\sf T}
-
*
-term. For this ‘pushing in’ of the second conjunct we
define an auxiliary function
f^{c}_{1}:P^{*}\times P^{\sf T}\to P^{*}
.

\displaystyle f^{c}(P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*},R^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}_{1}(Q^{*},R^{\sf T})

(18)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{1}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})

(19)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{1}(\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf T},Q^{\sf T})

(20)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{1}(P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{d},R^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}_{1}(Q^{d},R^{\sf T})

(21)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{1}(P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{c},R^{\sf T})

\displaystyle=P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}_{1}(Q^{c},R^{\sf T})

(22)

When the second conjunct is an
{\sf F}
-term, the result will naturally be an

{\sf F}
-term itself. So we need to convert the
{\sf T}
-
*
-term to an

{\sf F}
-term. Using (
FFEL4
) we reduce this problem to converting a

*
-term to an
{\sf F}
-term, for which we use the auxiliary function

f^{c}_{2}:P^{*}\times P^{\sf F}\to P^{\sf F}
.

\displaystyle f^{c}(P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*},R^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=f^{c}(P^{\sf T},f^{c}_{2}(Q^{*},R^{\sf F}))

(23)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{2}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},R^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf T},R^{\sf F})

(24)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{2}(\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{\sf T},R^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}(P^{\sf T},R^{\sf F})

(25)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{2}(P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{d},R^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=f^{c}_{2}(P^{*},f^{c}_{2}(Q^{d},R^{\sf F}))

(26)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{2}(P^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{c},R^{\sf F})

\displaystyle=f^{c}_{2}(P^{*},f^{c}_{2}(Q^{c},R^{\sf F}))

(27)

Finally we are left with conjunctions and disjunctions of two

{\sf T}
-
*
-terms, thus completing the definition of
f^{c}
. We use the
auxiliary function
f^{c}_{3}:P^{*}\times P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P^{*}\to P^{*}
to ensure
that the result is a
{\sf T}
-
*
-term.

\displaystyle f^{c}(P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*},R^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}S^{*})

\displaystyle=P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}f^{c}_{3}(Q^{*},R^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}S^{*})

(28)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{3}(P^{*},Q^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R^{\ell})

\displaystyle=f^{c}_{1}(P^{*},Q^{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R^{\ell}

(29)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{3}(P^{*},Q^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(R^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}S^{d}))

\displaystyle=f^{c}_{3}(P^{*},Q^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R^{*})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}S^{d}

(30)

\displaystyle f^{c}_{3}(P^{*},Q^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(R^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}S^{c}))

\displaystyle=f^{c}_{1}(P^{*},Q^{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(R^{*}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}S^{c})

(31)

As promised, we now define the normalization function
f:{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}\to\textup{FNF}

recursively, using
f^{n}
and
f^{c}
, as follows.

\displaystyle f(a)

\displaystyle={\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})

(32)

\displaystyle f({\sf T})

\displaystyle={\sf T}

(33)

\displaystyle f({\sf F})

\displaystyle={\sf F}

(34)

\displaystyle f(\neg P)

\displaystyle=f^{n}(f(P))

(35)

\displaystyle f(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=f^{c}(f(P),f(Q))

(36)

\displaystyle f(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=f^{n}(f^{c}(f^{n}(f(P)),f^{n}(f(Q))))

(37)

Theorem 2.9
.

For any
P\in{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
,
f(P)
terminates,
f(P)\in\textup{FNF}
and
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash f(P)=P
.

In Appendix
A
we first prove a number of lemmas showing that the
definitions
f^{n}
and
f^{c}
are correct and use those to prove the theorem.
The main reason to use a normalization function rather than a
term rewriting system to prove the correctness of
FNF
is that this relieves us
of the need to prove the confluence of the
induced rewriting system, thus simplifying the proof.

Tree Structure.

Below we prove that
EqFFEL
axiomatises
FFEL
by
showing that for
P\in\textup{FNF}
we can invert
\mathit{fe}(P)
. To do this we need to
prove several structural properties of the trees in the image of
\mathit{fe}
. In the
definition of
\mathit{fe}
we can see how
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
is assembled from

\mathit{fe}(P)
and
\mathit{fe}(Q)
and similarly for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. To decompose
these trees we introduce some notation. The trees in the image of
\mathit{fe}

are all finite binary trees over
A
with leaves in
\{{\sf T},{\sf F}\}
, i.e.,

\mathit{fe}[{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}]\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{A}
. We will now also consider the set
{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
of binary
trees over
A
with leaves in
\{{\sf T},{\sf F},\triangle\}
.
Similarly we consider
\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}
, the set of binary
trees over
A
with leaves in
\{{\sf T},{\sf F},\triangle_{1},\triangle_{2}\}
. The
\triangle
,

\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
will be used as placeholders when composing or
decomposing trees. Replacement of the leaves of trees in
{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}

by trees (either in
\mathcal{T}_{A}
,
{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
or
\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}
) is defined analogous to
replacement for trees in
\mathcal{T}_{A}
, adopting the same notational conventions.

For example we have by definition of
\mathit{fe}
that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
can be
decomposed as

\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}][\triangle_{1}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q),\triangle_{2}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]],

where
\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}]\in\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}
and
\mathit{fe}(Q)
and

\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]
are in
\mathcal{T}_{A}
. We note that this only works because
the trees in the image of
\mathit{fe}
, or more general, in
\mathcal{T}_{A}
, do not contain any
triangles. Similarly, as we discussed previously,
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf F})=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]
, which we can write as

\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle][\triangle\mapsto{\sf F}]
. We start by analysing the

\mathit{fe}
-image of
\ell
-terms.

Lemma 2.10

(Structure of
\ell
-terms)
.

There is no
\ell
-term
P
such that
\mathit{fe}(P)
can be decomposed as

X[\triangle\mapsto Y]
with
X\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
and
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
, where
X\neq\triangle
, but
does contain
\triangle
, and
Y
contains occurrences of both
{\sf T}
and

{\sf F}
.

Proof.

Let
P
be some
\ell
-term. When we analyse the grammar of
P
we find that
one branch from the root of
\mathit{fe}(P)
will only contain
{\sf T}
and not
{\sf F}

and the other branch vice versa. Hence if
\mathit{fe}(P)=X[\triangle\mapsto Y]
and
Y

contains occurrences of both
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
, then
Y
must contain the
root and hence
X=\triangle
.
∎

By definition a
*
-term contains at least one
\ell
-term and hence for any

*
-term
P
,
\mathit{fe}(P)
contains both
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
. The following lemma
provides the
\mathit{fe}
-image of the rightmost
\ell
-term in a
*
-term to witness
this fact.

Lemma 2.11

(Determinativeness)
.

For all
*
-terms
P
,
\mathit{fe}(P)
can be decomposed as
X[\triangle\mapsto Y]
with
X\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
and
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
such that
X
contains
\triangle
and
Y=\mathit{fe}(Q)
for
some
\ell
-term
Q
. Note that
X
may be
\triangle
. We will refer to
Y
as the
witness for this lemma for
P
.

Proof.

By induction on the complexity of
*
-terms
P
modulo the complexity of

\ell
-terms. In the base case
P
is an
\ell
-term and
\mathit{fe}(P)=\triangle[\triangle\mapsto\mathit{fe}(P)]
is the desired decomposition by Lemma
2.10
.
For the induction we have to consider both
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
and
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
.

We treat only the case for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
, the case for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

is analogous. Let
X[\triangle\mapsto Y]
be the decomposition for
\mathit{fe}(Q)
which we
have by induction hypothesis. Since by definition of
\mathit{fe}
on
{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}}
we
have

\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]],

we also have

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto X[\triangle\mapsto Y],{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto X,{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]][\triangle\mapsto Y],

where the second equality is due to the fact that the only triangles in

\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto X,{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]

are those occurring in
X
. This gives our desired decomposition.
∎

The following lemma illustrates another structural property of trees in the
image of
*
-terms under
\mathit{fe}
, namely that the left branch of any
determinative atom in such a tree is different from its right branch.

Lemma 2.12

(Non-decomposition)
.

There is no
*
-term
P
such that
\mathit{fe}(P)
can be decomposed as

X[\triangle\mapsto Y]
with
X\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
and
Y\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
, where
X\neq\triangle
and
X

contains
\triangle
, but not
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
.

Proof.

By induction on
P
modulo the complexity of
\ell
-terms. The base case covers

\ell
-terms and follows immediately from Lemma
2.11
(
\mathit{fe}(P)

contains occurrences of both
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
) and Lemma
2.10

(no non-trivial decomposition exists that contains both). For the induction
we assume that the lemma holds for all
*
-terms with lesser complexity than
P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
and
P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
.

We start with the case for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. Suppose for contradiction that

\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)=X[\triangle\mapsto Y]
with
X\neq\triangle
and
X
not containing
any occurrences of
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
. Let
R
be a witness of Lemma

2.11
for
P
. Now note that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
has a subtree

R[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]].

Because
Y
must contain both the occurrences of
{\sf F}
in the one branch
from the root of this subtree as well as the occurrences of
\mathit{fe}(Q)
in the
other (because they contain
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
), Lemma
2.10

implies that
Y
must (strictly) contain
\mathit{fe}(Q)
and

\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]
. Hence there is a
Z\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
such that
\mathit{fe}(P)=X[\triangle\mapsto Z]
, which violates the induction hypothesis. The case for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
proceeds analogously.
∎

We now arrive at two crucial definitions for our completeness proof. When
considering
*
-terms we already know that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
can be
decomposed as

\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}][\triangle_{1}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q),\triangle_{2}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]].

Our goal now is to give a definition for a type of decomposition so that this
is the only such decomposition for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. We also ensure that

\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
does not have a decomposition of that type, so that we can
distinguish
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
from
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. Similarly, we define
another type of decomposition so that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
can only be decomposed
as

\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}][\triangle_{1}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],\triangle_{2}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)]

and that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
does not have a decomposition of that type.

Definition 2.13
.

The pair
(Y,Z)\in\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}\times\mathcal{T}_{A}
is a
candidate conjunction
decomposition (ccd)
of
X\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
, if

•

X=Y[\triangle_{1}\mapsto Z,\triangle_{2}\mapsto Z[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]]
,

•

Y
contains both
\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
,

•

Y
contains neither
{\sf T}
nor
{\sf F}
, and

•

Z
contains both
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
.

Similarly,
(Y,Z)
is a
candidate disjunction decomposition (cdd)
of

X
, if

•

X=Y[\triangle_{1}\mapsto Z[{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],\triangle_{2}\mapsto Z]
,

•

Y
contains both
\triangle_{1}
and
\triangle_{2}
,

•

Y
contains neither
{\sf T}
nor
{\sf F}
, and

•

Z
contains both
{\sf T}
and
{\sf F}
.

The ccd and cdd are not necessarily the decompositions we are looking for,
because, for example,
\mathit{fe}((P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R)
has a ccd

(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}],\mathit{fe}(Q~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}R))
, whereas the
decomposition we need is
(\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}],\mathit{fe}(R))
. Therefore we refine these
definitions to obtain the decompositions we seek.

Definition 2.14
.

The pair
(Y,Z)\in\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}\times\mathcal{T}_{A}
is a
conjunction decomposition
(cd)
of
X\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
, if it is a ccd of
X
and there is no other ccd
(Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})

of
X
where the depth of
Z^{\prime}
is smaller than that of
Z
. Similarly,
(Y,Z)
is a
disjunction decomposition (dd)
of
X
, if it is a cdd of
X

and there is no other cdd
(Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
of
X
where the depth of
Z^{\prime}
is smaller
than that of
Z
.

Theorem 2.15
.

For any
*
-term
P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
, i.e., with
P\in P^{*}
and
Q\in P^{d}
,
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
has the (unique) cd

(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}],\mathit{fe}(Q))

and no dd. For any
*
-term
P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
, i.e., with
P\in P^{*}
and
Q\in P^{c}
,
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
has no cd and its (unique) dd is

(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}],\mathit{fe}(Q)).

Proof.

We first treat the case for
P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q
and start with cd. Note that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
has a ccd
(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}],\mathit{fe}(Q))

by definition of
\mathit{fe}
(for the first condition) and by Lemma
2.11

(for the fourth condition). It is immediate that it satisfies the second and
third conditions. It also follows that for any ccd
(Y,Z)
either
Z
contains
or is contained in
\mathit{fe}(Q)
, for suppose otherwise, then
Y
will contain an
occurrence of
{\sf T}
or of
{\sf F}
, namely those we know by
Lemma
2.11
that
\mathit{fe}(Q)
has. Therefore it suffices to show that
there is no ccd
(Y,Z)
where
Z
is strictly contained in
\mathit{fe}(Q)
. Suppose
for contradiction that
(Y,Z)
is such a ccd. If
Z
is strictly contained in

\mathit{fe}(Q)
we can decompose
\mathit{fe}(Q)
as
\mathit{fe}(Q)=V[\triangle\mapsto Z]
for some
V\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
that contains but is not equal to
\triangle
. By Lemma
2.12

this implies that
V
contains
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
. But then so does
Y
,
because

Y=\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto V[\triangle\mapsto\triangle_{1}],{\sf F}\mapsto V[\triangle\mapsto\triangle_{2}]],

and so
(Y,Z)
is not a ccd for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. Therefore

(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle_{1},{\sf F}\mapsto\triangle_{2}],\mathit{fe}(Q))
is the
unique
cd
for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
.

Now for the dd. It suffices to show that there is no cdd for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. Suppose for contradiction that
(Y,Z)
is a cdd for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
.
We note that
Z
cannot be contained in
\mathit{fe}(Q)
, for then by Lemma

2.12
,
Y
would contain
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
. So
Z
(strictly)
contains
\mathit{fe}(Q)
. But then because

Y[\triangle_{1}\mapsto Z[{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],\triangle_{2}\mapsto Z]=\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q),

we would have by Lemma
2.11
that
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
does not contain
an occurrence of
\mathit{fe}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]
. But the cd of
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

tells us that it does, contradiction! Therefore there is no cdd, and hence no
dd, for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. The case for
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
proceeds
analogously.
∎

At this point we have the tools necessary to invert
\mathit{fe}
on
*
-terms, at
least down to the level of
\ell
-terms. We note that we can easily detect if a
tree in the image of
\mathit{fe}
is in the image of
P^{\ell}
, because all leaves to
the left of the root are one truth value, while all the leaves to the right are
the other. To invert
\mathit{fe}
on
{\sf T}
-
*
-terms we still need to be able to
reconstruct
\mathit{fe}(P^{\sf T})
and
\mathit{fe}(Q^{*})
from
\mathit{fe}(P^{\sf T}~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q^{*})
. To
this end we define a
{\sf T}
-
*
-decomposition.

Definition 2.16
.

The pair
(Y,Z)\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}\times\mathcal{T}_{A}
is a
{\sf T}
-
*
-decomposition
(tsd)
of
X\in\mathcal{T}_{A}
, if
X=Y[\triangle\mapsto Z]
,
Y
does not contain
{\sf T}
or

{\sf F}
and there is no decomposition
(V,W)\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}\times\mathcal{T}_{A}
of
Z
such
that

•

Z=V[\triangle\mapsto W]
,

•

V
contains
\triangle
,

•

V\neq\triangle
, and

•

V
contains neither
{\sf T}
nor
{\sf F}
.

Theorem 2.17
.

For any
{\sf T}
-term
P
and
*
-term
Q
the (unique) tsd of
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
is

(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle],\mathit{fe}(Q)).

Proof.

First we observe that
(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle],\mathit{fe}(Q))
is a tsd because by
definition of
\mathit{fe}
on
~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}
we have
\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}(Q)]=\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
and
\mathit{fe}(Q)
is non-decomposable by Lemma
2.12
.

Suppose for contradiction that there is another tsd
(Y,Z)
of
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
. Now
Z
must contain or be contained in
\mathit{fe}(Q)
for otherwise

Y
would contain
{\sf T}
or
{\sf F}
, i.e., the ones we know
\mathit{fe}(Q)
has by
Lemma
2.11
.

If
Z
is strictly contained in
\mathit{fe}(Q)
, then
\mathit{fe}(Q)=V[\triangle\mapsto Z]
for
some
V\in{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}
with
V\neq\triangle
and
V
not containing
{\sf T}
or

{\sf F}
(because then
Y
would too). But this violates Lemma

2.12
, which states that no such decomposition exists. If
Z

strictly contains
\mathit{fe}(Q)
, then
Z
contains at least one atom from
P
. But
the left branch of any atom in
\mathit{fe}(P)
is equal to its right branch and hence

Z
is decomposable. Therefore
(\mathit{fe}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\triangle],\mathit{fe}(Q))
is the

unique
tsd of
\mathit{fe}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)
.
∎

Completeness.

With the last two theorems we can prove completeness for

FFEL
. We define three auxiliary functions to aid in our definition of the
inverse of
\mathit{fe}
on
FNF
. Let
\textup{cd}:\mathcal{T}_{A}\to\mathcal{T}_{A,1,2}\times\mathcal{T}_{A}
be the function
that returns the conjunction decomposition of its argument,
dd
of the same
type its disjunction decomposition and
\textup{tsd}:\mathcal{T}_{A}\to{\mathcal{T}_{A,\triangle}}\times\mathcal{T}_{A}
its

{\sf T}
-
*
-decomposition. Naturally, these functions are undefined when their
argument does not have a decomposition of the specified type. Each of these
functions returns a pair and we will use
\textup{cd}_{1}
(
\textup{dd}_{1}
,
\textup{tsd}_{1}
) to denote
the first element of this pair and
\textup{cd}_{2}
(
\textup{dd}_{2}
,
\textup{tsd}_{2}
) to denote the
second element.

We define
g:\mathcal{T}_{A}\to{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
using the functions
g^{\sf T}:\mathcal{T}_{A}\to{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
for
inverting trees in the image of
{\sf T}
-terms and
g^{\sf F}
,
g^{\ell}

and
g^{*}
of the same type for inverting trees in the image of

{\sf F}
-terms,
\ell
-terms and
*
-terms, respectively. These functions are
defined as follows.

\displaystyle g^{\sf T}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\sf T}&\textrm{if $X={\sf T}$}\\
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{\sf T}(Y)&\textrm{if $X=Y\unlhd a\unrhd Z$}\end{cases}

(38)

We note that we might as well have used the right branch from the
root in the recursive case.
We chose the left branch here to more closely
mirror the definition of the corresponding function for Free short-circuit logic as
defined in
[
19
,
18
]
.

\displaystyle g^{\sf F}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\sf F}&\textrm{if $X={\sf F}$}\\
a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{\sf F}(Z)&\textrm{if $X=Y\unlhd a\unrhd Z$}\end{cases}

(39)

Similarly, we could have taken the left branch in this case.

\displaystyle g^{\ell}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{\sf T}(Y)&\textrm{if $X=Y\unlhd a\unrhd Z$ for some $a\in A$}\\
&\textrm{and $Y$ only has ${\sf T}$-leaves}\\
\neg a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{\sf T}(Z)&\textrm{if $X=Y\unlhd a\unrhd Z$ for some $a\in A$}\\
&\textrm{and $Z$ only has ${\sf T}$-leaves}\end{cases}

(40)

\displaystyle g^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}g^{*}(\textup{cd}_{1}(X)[\triangle_{1}\mapsto{\sf T},\triangle_{2}\mapsto{\sf F}])~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{*}(\textup{cd}_{2}(X))&\textrm{if $X$ has a cd}\\
g^{*}(\textup{dd}_{1}(X)[\triangle_{1}\mapsto{\sf T},\triangle_{2}\mapsto{\sf F}])~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{*}(\textup{dd}_{2}(X))&\textrm{if $X$ has a dd}\\
g^{\ell}(X)&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

(41)

We can immediately see how Theorem
2.15
will be used in
the correctness proof of
g^{*}
.

\displaystyle g(X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}g^{\sf T}(X)&\textrm{if $X$ has only ${\sf T}$-leaves}\\
g^{\sf F}(X)&\textrm{if $X$ has only ${\sf F}$-leaves}\\
g^{\sf T}(\textup{tsd}_{1}(X)[\triangle\mapsto{\sf T}])~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}g^{*}(\textup{tsd}_{2}(X))&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

(42)

Similarly, we can see how Theorem
2.17
is used in the correctness
proof of
g
. It should come as no surprise that
g
is indeed correct
and inverts
\mathit{fe}
on
FNF
.

Theorem 2.18
.

For all
P\in\textup{FNF}
,
g(\mathit{fe}(P))=P
, i.e.
g(\mathit{fe}(P))
is syntactically equal to
P
for
P\in\textup{FNF}
.

The proof for this theorem can be found in Appendix
A
. For the
sake of completeness, we separately state the completeness result below.

Theorem 2.19

(Completeness)
.

For all
P,Q\in{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
, if
\textup{$\textrm{FFEL}$}\vDash P=Q
then
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash P=Q
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that for
P,Q\in\textup{FNF}
,
\mathit{fe}(P)=\mathit{fe}(Q)
implies
P=Q
, i.e.,
P
and
Q
are syntactically equal.
To see this suppose that
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}
are two
FEL
-terms and

\mathit{fe}(P^{\prime})=\mathit{fe}(Q^{\prime})
. By Theorem
2.9
,
P^{\prime}
is derivably equal to an
FNF
-term

P
, i.e.,
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash P^{\prime}=P
, and
Q^{\prime}
is derivably equal to an

FNF
-term
Q
. Lemma
2.7
then
gives us
\mathit{fe}(P^{\prime})=\mathit{fe}(P)
and
\mathit{fe}(Q^{\prime})=\mathit{fe}(Q)
, and thus
\mathit{fe}(P)=\mathit{fe}(Q)
. Hence
by Theorem
2.18
,

P=Q
, so in particular
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash P=Q
. Transitivity then gives
us
\textup{$\textrm{EqFFEL}$}\vdash P^{\prime}=Q^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

3
Free FEL with undefinedness:
\textup{$\textrm{FFEL}$}^{\sf U}

In this section we define
\textup{$\textrm{FFEL}$}^{\sf U}
, the extension of
FFEL
with the truth value
undefined
,
for which we use the constant
{\sf U}
.
Evaluation trees with undefinedness were introduced in
[
6
]
, where they serve as
a semantics for short-circuit logic with undefinedness: the evaluation/interpretation of each
atom may be undefined, i.e. not yield a classical truth value (
true
or
false
).
Well-known three-valued extensions of propositional logic
are Kleene’s ‘strong’ three-valued logic
[
14
]
,
in which evaluation is executed in parallel so that
{\sf F}\wedge x=x\wedge{\sf F}={\sf F}
,
Bochvar’s ‘strict’ logic
[
8
]
with a constant
\mathsf{N}
for ‘nonsense’ or ‘meaningless’, in which an expression has the value
\mathsf{N}
as soon as it has a component
with that value, and McCarthy’s ‘sequential’ logic
[
15
]
, in which evaluation proceeds sequentially
from left to right so that
{\sf F}\wedge{\sf U}={\sf F}
and
{\sf U}\wedge{\sf F}={\sf U}
.
We refer to Bergstra, Bethke, and Rodenburg
[
1
, Sect.2]
for a brief discussion of these logics.
Here we provide equational axioms for the equality of evaluation trees for full left-sequential evaluation
with undefinedness and prove a completeness result.

Definition 3.1
.

The set
{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}}
of
\bm{{\sf U}}
-evaluation trees
over
A
with leaves in

\{{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U}\}
is defined inductively by

{\sf T}\in{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}},\hskip 10.00002pt{\sf F}\in{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}},\hskip 10.00002pt{\sf U}\in{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}},\hskip 10.00002pt(X\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd Y)\in{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}}~\text{ for any }X,Y\in{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}}\text{ and }a\in A.

The operator
\_\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd\_
is called

\bm{{\sf U}}
-tree composition over
a
.
In the evaluation tree
X\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd Y
,
the root is represented by
\underline{a}
,
the left branch by
X
, the right branch by
Y
, and the underlining of the root
represents a middle branch to the leaf
{\sf U}
.

Next to the formal notation for evaluation
trees we again introduce a more pictorial representation. For example,
the tree

({\sf F}\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd{\sf F})\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd({\sf T}\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd{\sf F})

can be represented as follows, where
\unlhd
yields a left branch, and
\unrhd
a right branch:

a
a
{\sf F}
{\sf U}
{\sf F}
{\sf U}
a
{\sf T}
{\sf U}
{\sf F}

(Picture 2)

We extend the set
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}
to
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}^{\!{\sf U}}
of (left-sequential) propositional expressions over
A

with
{\sf U}
by the following grammar (
a\in A
):

P::={\sf T}\mid{\sf F}\mid{\sf U}\mid a\mid\neg P\mid P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P\mid P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}P

and refer to its signature by

\Sigma_{\textup{$\textrm{FEL}$}}^{\sf U}(A)=\{~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}},\neg,{\sf T},{\sf F},{\sf U},a\mid a\in A\}.

We interpret propositional expressions in
{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}^{\!{\sf U}}
as evaluation trees
by extending the function
\mathit{fe}
(Definition
2.2
).

Definition 3.2
.

The unary
full evaluation function

\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}:{\mathcal{SP}_{\!A}}^{\!{\sf U}}\to{\mathcal{T}_{A}^{\sf U}}

is defined as follows, where
a\in A
:

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}({\sf T})

\displaystyle={\sf T},~~\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}({\sf F})={\sf F},

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(\neg P)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F},{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}({\sf U})

\displaystyle={\sf U},

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(Q),{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(Q)[{\sf T}\mapsto{\sf F}]],

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(a)

\displaystyle={\sf T}\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd{\sf F},

\displaystyle\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(P~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}Q)

\displaystyle=\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(P)[{\sf T}\mapsto\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(Q)[{\sf F}\mapsto{\sf T}],{\sf F}\mapsto\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(Q)].

Two examples: the evaluation trees
\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}(a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf U})={\sf U}\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd{\sf U}
and

\mathit{fe}^{\sf U}((a~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.0){$\vee$}\put(-0.42,1.4){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}{\sf T})~\mathbin{\begin{picture}(-0.3,0.0)\put(-0.6,0.2){$\wedge$}\put(-0.46,-0.0){\circle*{0.6}}\end{picture}}b)=({\sf T}\unlhd\underline{b}\unrhd{\sf F})\\
\unlhd\underline{a}\unrhd({\sf T}\unlhd\underline{b}\unrhd{\sf F})

can be depicted as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{array}[]{l}\hbox to98.9pt{\vbox to33.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 49.44962pt\lower-28.14479pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
